  uid=1_49 heading "AutoHarness: improving LLM agents by automatically synthesizing a code harness" level="1"
  uid=1_50 StaticText "Xinghua Lou, Miguel Lázaro-Gredilla, Antoine Dedieu,"
  uid=1_51 LineBreak "
"
  uid=1_52 StaticText "Carter Wendelken, Wolfgang Lehrach, Kevin P. Murphy "
  uid=1_53 StaticText "Google DeepMind "
  uid=1_54 StaticText "{xinghua,lazarogredilla,adedieu,cwendelken,wpl,kpmurphy}@deepmind.com"
  uid=1_55 heading "Abstract" level="6"
  uid=1_56 StaticText "Despite significant strides in language models in the last few years, when used as agents, such models often try to perform actions that are not just suboptimal for a given state, but are strictly prohibited by the external environment. For example, in the recent Kaggle GameArena chess competition, 78% of Gemini-2.5-Flash losses were attributed to illegal moves. Often people manually write "harnesses" around LLMs to prevent such failures. In this paper, we demonstrate that Gemini-2.5-Flash can automatically synthesize such a code harness, using a small number of rounds of iterative code refinement given feedback from the (game) environment. The resulting harness prevents all illegal moves in 145 different TextArena games (both 1-player and 2-player), enabling the smaller Gemini-2.5-Flash model to outperform larger models, such as Gemini-2.5-Pro. Pushing our technique to the limit, we can get Gemini-2.5-Flash to generate the entire policy in code, thus eliminating the need to use the LLM at decision making time. The resulting code-policy receives a higher average reward than Gemini-2.5-Pro and GPT-5.2-High on 16 TextArena 1-player games. Our results show that using a smaller model to synthesize a custom code harness (or entire policy) can outperform a much larger model, while also being more cost effective."
  uid=1_57 heading "1 Introduction" level="2"
  uid=1_58 StaticText "Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in code synthesis and solving math problems (see e.g., "
  uid=1_59 StaticText "Chervonyi"

    uid=1_66 StaticText "2025"
  uid=1_67 StaticText ")"
  uid=1_68 StaticText "). However, their planning and reasoning performance can be brittle (see e.g., "
  uid=1_69 StaticText "(Valmeekam"
  uid=1_70 StaticText " et al."
  uid=1_71 StaticText ", "
  uid=1_72 link "2023a" description="On the planning abilities of large language models - a critical investigation" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#bib.bib18"
    uid=1_73 StaticText "2023a"
  uid=1_74 StaticText "; Petrov"
  uid=1_75 StaticText " et al."
  uid=1_76 StaticText ", "
  uid=1_77 link "2025" description="Proof or bluff? evaluating llms on 2025 usa math olympiad" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#bib.bib10"
    uid=1_78 StaticText "2025"
  uid=1_79 StaticText ")"
  uid=1_80 StaticText ". For example, in the recent Kaggle GameArena "
  uid=1_81 StaticText "(Kaggle, "
  uid=1_82 link "2025" description="Kaggle game arena: a benchmarking platform for ai models" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#bib.bib16"
    uid=1_83 StaticText "2025"
  uid=1_84 StaticText ")"
  uid=1_85 StaticText " chess competition, 78% of losses by Gemini 2.5 Flash were attributed not to strategic blunders, but to simple illegal moves."
  uid=1_86 StaticText "This failure mode highlights a disconnect between the model’s apparent understanding of the game and its ability to actually follow the rules (see e.g. Fig. A16 in "
  uid=1_87 StaticText "(Ruoss"
  uid=1_88 StaticText " et al."
  uid=1_89 StaticText ", "
  uid=1_90 link "2024" description="LMAct: a benchmark for in-context imitation learning with long multimodal demonstrations" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#bib.bib19"
    uid=1_91 StaticText "2024"
  uid=1_92 StaticText ")"
  uid=1_93 StaticText ")."
  uid=1_94 StaticText "1"
  uid=1_95 StaticText "1"
  uid=1_96 StaticText "The general problem of knowing which actions are valid in a given state is called the ”action applicability” problem, and has been studied in the AI planning community "
  uid=1_97 StaticText "(Kokel"
  uid=1_98 StaticText " et al."
  uid=1_99 StaticText ", "
  uid=1_100 link "2025" description="ACPBench hard: unrestrained reasoning about action, change, and planning" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#bib.bib17"
    uid=1_101 StaticText "2025"
  uid=1_102 StaticText ")"
  uid=1_103 StaticText "."
  uid=1_104 StaticText " Traditional approaches to mitigate this involve fine-tuning on game trajectories or using hand-coded harnesses that verify the validity of a move. Fine-tuning LLMs, particularly at the scale of current flagship models, is neither fast nor cost effective, and can degrade model performance on other tasks, e.g. instruction following. Hand-designed harnesses are brittle and labor-intensive, requiring additional work for every new game. A more scalable solution — which we pursue in this paper — is to leverage the LLM’s own code-generation capabilities to bridge this gap."
  uid=1_105 StaticText "An agent is often defined as the combination of a specific LLM and a harness that acts as the “glue” or “plumbing” between the model and the task that needs to be solved. In this work, we propose “code as harness”, a framework where the LLM itself completes the agent by coding its own harness. In its simplest incarnation, the harness can be seen as a control loop that calls the LLM and rejects unacceptable answers. The definition of what is acceptable is itself learned. This essentially results in a rejection sampler for LLMs in which the conditioning is learned based on the task."
  uid=1_106 StaticText "We formulate the generation of this harness as a search problem over the space of programs. Unlike simple iterative prompting, we employ a tree search guided by Thompson sampling "
  uid=1_107 StaticText "(Tang"
  uid=1_108 StaticText " et al."
  uid=1_109 StaticText ", "
  uid=1_110 link "2024" description="Code repair with llms gives an exploration-exploitation tradeoff" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#bib.bib1"
    uid=1_111 StaticText "2024"
  uid=1_112 StaticText ")"
  uid=1_113 StaticText " to efficiently explore the landscape of potential harnesses. In this setup, the LLM acts as a mutation operator, proposing refinements to the code based on feedback from execution. The search algorithm balances exploration (trying distinct logic structures) and exploitation (refining a partially working harness) to converge on a robust control loop. The harness template can be more constrained (e.g., a fixed rejection sampling loop where we only learn a conditioning function with signature "
  uid=1_114 StaticText "def is_legal_action()"
  uid=1_115 StaticText "), or less so, with maximum flexibility resulting in a code-as-policy setup "
  uid=1_116 StaticText "(Liang"
  uid=1_117 StaticText " et al."
  uid=1_118 StaticText ", "
  uid=1_119 link "2023" description="Code as policies: language model programs for embodied control" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#bib.bib3"
    uid=1_120 StaticText "2023"
  uid=1_121 StaticText ")"
  uid=1_122 StaticText " in which code proposes the next action directly and no LLM calls are needed at execution time."
  uid=1_123 heading "2 Related work" level="2"
  uid=1_124 heading "LLMs for game playing and reasoning" level="5"

  uid=1_143 StaticText ")"
  uid=1_144 StaticText " to improve strategic planning. However, recent benchmarks reveal that even advanced models struggle with state tracking and validity in strictly defined environments "
  uid=1_145 StaticText "(Valmeekam"
  uid=1_146 StaticText " et al."
  uid=1_147 StaticText ", "
  uid=1_148 link "2023b" description="On the planning abilities of large language models-a critical investigation" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#bib.bib14"

    uid=1_156 StaticText "2023"
  uid=1_157 StaticText ")"
  uid=1_158 StaticText " utilize search during inference to simulate lookahead, but they rely on the LLM’s internal world model, which is prone to hallucination regarding valid transitions. Our work differs by offloading the state-transition validity checker to an external, verifiable program rather than relying on the model’s internal simulation. LLMs can also be used to generate code for the entire state transition function (i.e. world model) for a game "
  uid=1_159 StaticText "(Lehrach"
  uid=1_160 StaticText " et al."
  uid=1_161 StaticText ", "

    uid=1_163 StaticText "2025"
  uid=1_164 StaticText ")"
  uid=1_165 StaticText ", but that is unnecessarily onerous for complex games in which a comparatively simple strategy can be applied. In addition, this approach does not leverage the strategic abilities of the LLM to select between valid actions."
  uid=1_166 heading "Code as policy" level="5"
  uid=1_167 StaticText "Our approach builds upon the growing body of work using code generation for action planning. Voyager "
  uid=1_168 StaticText "(Wang"
  uid=1_169 StaticText " et al."
  uid=1_170 StaticText ", "
  uid=1_171 link "2023" description="Voyager: an open-ended embodied agent with large language models" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#bib.bib2"
    uid=1_172 StaticText "2023"
  uid=1_173 StaticText ")"
  uid=1_174 StaticText " demonstrated that LLMs could continuously learn Minecraft skills by storing executable code in a library. Similarly, Eureka "
  uid=1_175 StaticText "(Ma"
  uid=1_176 StaticText " et al."
  uid=1_177 StaticText ", "
  uid=1_178 link "2024" description="Eureka: human-level reward design via coding large language models" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#bib.bib4"
    uid=1_179 StaticText "2024"

  uid=1_182 StaticText "(Liang"
  uid=1_183 StaticText " et al."
  uid=1_184 StaticText ", "

  uid=1_191 heading "Refinement and search" level="5"
  uid=1_192 StaticText "As mentioned, iterative refinement is crucial for code generation. Reflexion "
  uid=1_193 StaticText "(Shinn"

  uid=1_200 StaticText "(Li"
  uid=1_201 StaticText " et al."
  uid=1_202 StaticText ", "
  uid=1_203 link "2022" description="Competition-level code generation with alphacode" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#bib.bib5"

  uid=1_212 StaticText ")"
  uid=1_213 StaticText " applies an evolutionary algorithm to entire codebases using an LLM as a mutation function. Our method integrates these concepts into a structured tree search using Thompson sampling, following "
  uid=1_214 StaticText "(Tang"
  uid=1_215 StaticText " et al."
  uid=1_216 StaticText ", "
  uid=1_217 link "2024" description="Code repair with llms gives an exploration-exploitation tradeoff" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#bib.bib1"

  uid=1_221 heading "3 Method" level="2"
  uid=1_222 image "Refer to caption" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329v1/x1.png"
  uid=1_223 StaticText "Figure 1:"
  uid=1_224 StaticText "Code-as-harness learning process."
  uid=1_225 StaticText "Inspired by "
  uid=1_226 StaticText "Tang"
  uid=1_227 StaticText " et al."
  uid=1_228 StaticText " ("
  uid=1_229 link "2024" description="Code repair with llms gives an exploration-exploitation tradeoff" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#bib.bib1"
    uid=1_230 StaticText "2024"
  uid=1_231 StaticText ")"
  uid=1_232 StaticText ", our approach maintains multiple code hypotheses in a tree structure, and uses Thompson sampling to choose which node to refine next, where the heuristic value for each node is the average legal move accuracy. The refinement (gradient-free code optimizer) is done with a base LLM, given feedback from the environment (critic) about whether the previous attempted moves were legal or not, and what reward they produced (if any), see Fig."
  uid=1_233 link "1" description="Figure 1 ‣ 3 Method ‣ AutoHarness: improving LLM agents by automatically synthesizing a code harness" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#S3.F1"
    uid=1_234 StaticText "1"
  uid=1_235 StaticText ". If "
  uid=1_236 StaticText "is_legal_action()"
  uid=1_237 StaticText " returns "
  uid=1_238 StaticText "True"
  uid=1_239 StaticText " but the action is invalid, we refine both functions; while if "
  uid=1_240 StaticText "is_legal_action()"
  uid=1_241 StaticText " returns "
  uid=1_242 StaticText "False"
  uid=1_243 StaticText " and the action is invalid, we only refine "
  uid=1_244 StaticText "propose_action()"
  uid=1_245 StaticText "."
  uid=1_246 StaticText "We can use this approach to generate different kinds of code harnesses: "
  uid=1_247 StaticText "harness-as-action-filter"
  uid=1_248 StaticText " calls "
  uid=1_249 StaticText "propose_action()"
  uid=1_250 StaticText " to generate a "
  uid=1_251 StaticText "set"
  uid=1_252 StaticText " of legal moves, and leverages the LLM to rank them (potentially using chain of thought reasoning); "
  uid=1_253 StaticText "harness-as-action-verifier"
  uid=1_254 StaticText " first calls the LLM to generate an action, verifies it by "
  uid=1_255 StaticText "is_legal_action()"
  uid=1_256 StaticText ", and, if invalid, repeats the process with a new prompt that includes an “illegal action” warning message; "
  uid=1_257 StaticText "harness-as-policy"
  uid=1_258 StaticText " uses code to choose the action; the code could in principle call an LLM, but in our setting, the policy just uses primitive Python functions and standard libraries such as numpy, so we do not need to invoke an LLM at inference time. In this paper, we mostly focus on the harness-as-action-verifier, but in Sec."
  uid=1_259 link "4.3" description="4.3 Harness-as-Policy ‣ 4 Experimental results ‣ AutoHarness: improving LLM agents by automatically synthesizing a code harness" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#S4.SS3"
    uid=1_260 StaticText "4.3"
  uid=1_261 StaticText ", we also report preliminary results on harness-as-policy."
  uid=1_262 heading "4 Experimental results" level="2"
  uid=1_263 StaticText "For our experiments we select all the 1-player (1P) and 2-player (2P) games from TextArena "
  uid=1_264 StaticText "(Guertler"
  uid=1_265 StaticText " et al."
  uid=1_266 StaticText ", "
  uid=1_267 link "2025" description="TextArena" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#bib.bib7"
    uid=1_268 StaticText "2025"
  uid=1_269 StaticText ")"
  uid=1_270 StaticText ", a large collection of complex and diverse text games, but exclude the 9 games whose action space is free-form text / dialog (such as "Mafia" and "Codenames"). This leaves us with 145 games, including well-known games — such as Chess, Checkers, Blackjack and Sudoku — as well as novel variants of these games. A full list of the games we use is in Appendix Table "
  uid=1_271 StaticText "LABEL:tab:long"
  uid=1_272 StaticText "."
  uid=1_273 StaticText "To make the problem more challenging for our harness, we modified some games by manually removing any form of “Available Moves” hints in the observation string (see Appendix Sec. "
  uid=1_274 link "A.4" description="A.4 Example game: Chess-v0 ‣ Appendix A TextArena games ‣ AutoHarness: improving LLM agents by automatically synthesizing a code harness" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#A1.SS4"
    uid=1_275 StaticText "A.4"
  uid=1_276 StaticText " for an example). We believe this better reflects many real-world scenarios where the agent needs to deduce legal actions from environmental feedback, rather than being told them explicitly. (Without this modification, the harness can just copy the list of legal actions from the prompt. This gives better results, but we show that it is unnecessary.)"
  uid=1_277 heading "4.1 Training" level="3"
  uid=1_278 image "Refer to caption" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329v1/x2.png"
  uid=1_279 StaticText "Figure 2:"
  uid=1_280 StaticText "Fraction of legal moves vs number of code refinements for a selection of 6 games."
  uid=1_281 StaticText "Our training setup (for harness-as-action-verifier) is as follows. At each iteration, we use 10 parallel environments and roll out to at most 1000 steps (with auto environment resetting). Rollout is terminated whenever an illegal move is made by the code or code execution fails. At most 5 failed steps are sampled and fed to the "
  uid=1_282 StaticText "Critic"
  uid=1_283 StaticText ", which consolidates various types of errors. These steps with error messages, together with the original code, are fed into the "
  uid=1_284 StaticText "Refiner"
  uid=1_285 StaticText " to generate new (hopefully improved) code. We set heuristic weight to 1.0 for Thompson sampling. Training ends when the heuristic value (i.e. the legal action success rate) reaches 1.0, or we time out. We use Gemini-2.5-Flash for training."
  uid=1_286 StaticText "On average, training ends after 14.5 tree search iterations, while 19/32 games end in less than 10 iterations. The games that required the most number of LLM calls to learn are are GermanWhist-v0 (2P), Cryptarithm-v0 (1P), Othello-v0 (2P) and Chess-v0 (2P), as shown in Fig "
  uid=1_287 link "2" description="Figure 2 ‣ 4.1 Training ‣ 4 Experimental results ‣ AutoHarness: improving LLM agents by automatically synthesizing a code harness" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#S4.F2"
    uid=1_288 StaticText "2"
  uid=1_289 StaticText ". We measure the accuracy of the action filter by applying it to novel test rollouts (of length 1000, across 10 random random seeds per game), and measuring the fraction of legal actions. We achieved 100% legal action success rate for all the games as shown in Appendix Table "
  uid=1_290 StaticText "LABEL:tab:long"
  uid=1_291 StaticText ". See Appendix Sec. "
  uid=1_292 link "D" description="Appendix D Sample Harness Code Snippets ‣ AutoHarness: improving LLM agents by automatically synthesizing a code harness" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#A4"
    uid=1_293 StaticText "D"
  uid=1_294 StaticText " for examples of the generated code harness."
  uid=1_295 heading "4.2 Evaluation" level="3"
  uid=1_296 StaticText "We now turn to evaluating performance of agents during actual game play. For reasons of efficiency, we focus our results on 16 1P games and 16 2P games, rather than using all 145 games. We evaluate the following agents: Gemini-2.5-Flash, Gemini-2.5-Pro and Gemini-2.5-Flash+Harness (ours)."
  uid=1_297 StaticText "2"
  uid=1_298 StaticText "2"
  uid=1_299 StaticText "Note that our method first uses an LLM (here Gemini-2.5-Flash) to generate the action verifier code harness, and then uses this harness to filter proposals from the same LLM."
  uid=1_300 StaticText " We use the same optimized prompt in all experiments. For 1P games, we run 20 matches and use the reward as the evaluation metric. For 2P games, we run 40 matches with random seeds, split evenly between our method being the first or second player, and we use the average win/draw/loss rate as the evaluation metrics."
  uid=1_301 image "Refer to caption" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329v1/x3.png"
  uid=1_302 StaticText "Figure 3:"
  uid=1_303 StaticText "Win/lose/draw rate of our method vs Gemini-2.5-Pro for each of the 16 2P games."
  uid=1_304 StaticText "We show results for 2P games in Fig. "
  uid=1_305 link "3" description="Figure 3 ‣ 4.2 Evaluation ‣ 4 Experimental results ‣ AutoHarness: improving LLM agents by automatically synthesizing a code harness" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#S4.F3"
    uid=1_306 StaticText "3"
  uid=1_307 StaticText ". We see that our approach enables a much smaller Gemini-2.5-Flash to win 9/16 games (overall win rate of 56.3%) against a much larger Gemini-2.5-Pro (overall win rate of 38.2%). When playing against (vanilla) Gemini-2.5-Flash, we win 12/16 games, and the overall win rate rises to 64.8%."
  uid=1_308 image "Refer to caption" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329v1/x4.png"
  uid=1_309 StaticText "Figure 4:"
  uid=1_310 StaticText "Average reward of our method and Gemini-2.5-Pro for each of the 16 1P games."
  uid=1_311 StaticText "We show results for 1P games in Fig. "
  uid=1_312 link "4" description="Figure 4 ‣ 4.2 Evaluation ‣ 4 Experimental results ‣ AutoHarness: improving LLM agents by automatically synthesizing a code harness" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#S4.F4"
    uid=1_313 StaticText "4"
  uid=1_314 StaticText ". We see that our approach achieves a higher reward than Gemini-2.5-Pro in 8/16 games, and ties in 5/16 games. On average, we achieve 0.745 reward, in comparison to 0.707 (Gemini-2.5-Pro) and 0.673 (Gemini-2.5-Flash)."
  uid=1_315 heading "4.3 Harness-as-Policy" level="3"
  uid=1_316 StaticText "As an extreme case, we consider learning the entire policy as code, dispensing with the need to use an LLM at test time. We evaluate this on 16 1P games (since it is much harder to learn an entire policy in code form for 2P games"
  uid=1_317 StaticText "3"
  uid=1_318 StaticText "3"
  uid=1_319 StaticText "Two-player games require strategic reasoning about the opponent’s policy which often requires MCTS-like methods at run time (see e.g., "
  uid=1_320 StaticText "(Duan"
  uid=1_321 StaticText " et al."
  uid=1_322 StaticText ", "
  uid=1_323 link "2024" description="GTBench: uncovering the strategic reasoning limitations of LLMs via game-theoretic evaluations" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#bib.bib20"
    uid=1_324 StaticText "2024"
  uid=1_325 StaticText ")"
  uid=1_326 StaticText "). While in principle our code synthesis method could generate such a policy, it would also need to learn a code world model to search over, as in "
  uid=1_327 StaticText "(Lehrach"
  uid=1_328 StaticText " et al."
  uid=1_329 StaticText ", "
  uid=1_330 link "2025" description="Code world models for general game playing" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#bib.bib15"
    uid=1_331 StaticText "2025"
  uid=1_332 StaticText ")"
  uid=1_333 StaticText ", which is challenging for text games."
  uid=1_334 StaticText ".) In addition to the above agents, we evaluate three new agents: GPT-5.2 (no thinking), GPT-5.2-High (high thinking) and Harness-as-Policy (ours). All agents are evaluated 20 times per game, as before, except for GPT-5.2 and GPT-5.2-High, which are repeated 10 and 5 times, for cost reasons."
  uid=1_335 image "Refer to caption" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329v1/x5.png"
  uid=1_336 StaticText "Figure 5:"
  uid=1_337 StaticText "Average reward of different agents across 16 TextArena 1P games."
  uid=1_338 StaticText "For training, we modify the heuristic value to include the reward. Specifially we set "
  uid=1_339 StaticText "𝐻"
  uid=1_340 StaticText "="
  uid=1_341 StaticText "0"
  uid=1_342 StaticText " if an illegal action is taken, and "
  uid=1_343 StaticText "𝐻"
  uid=1_344 StaticText "="
  uid=1_345 StaticText "0.5"
  uid=1_346 StaticText "+"
  uid=1_347 StaticText "0.5"
  uid=1_348 StaticText "​"
  uid=1_349 StaticText "𝑟"
  uid=1_350 StaticText " otherwise, where "
  uid=1_351 StaticText "𝑟"
  uid=1_352 StaticText "∈"
  uid=1_353 StaticText "["
  uid=1_354 StaticText "0.0"
  uid=1_355 StaticText ","
  uid=1_356 StaticText "1.0"
  uid=1_357 StaticText "]"
  uid=1_358 StaticText " is the environment reward, which is only available at the end of the trajectory (sparse reward setting). We train Harness-as-Policy using our code synthesis method with Gemini-2.5-Flash to a maximum of 256 iterations. On average, training takes 89.4 iterations and achieves a heuristic value of 0.939."
  uid=1_359 StaticText "As shown in Fig. "
  uid=1_360 link "5" description="Figure 5 ‣ 4.3 Harness-as-Policy ‣ 4 Experimental results ‣ AutoHarness: improving LLM agents by automatically synthesizing a code harness" url="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.03329#S4.F5"
    uid=1_361 StaticText "5"
  uid=1_362 StaticText ", our approach achieves the highest average reward (0.870), outperforming all other agents including GPT-5.2 (0.635), Gemini-2.5-Pro (0.707), and GPT-5.2-High (0.844). Per game, we win 3/16 games while GPT-5.2-High wins 5/16, and we tie the remaining 8/16 (details in the appendix). Since Harness-as-Policy generates pure (Python) code, our test time cost is nearly zero, while the GPT-5.2 and GPT-5.2-High experiments cost approximately $640."
  uid=1_363 heading "5 Conclusion and Future Work" level="2"
  uid=1_364 StaticText "We developed a novel approach for improving the performance of an LLM agent, based on automatically synthesizing a code harness. Currently we generate a separate harness for each environment (game). In the future, we would like to distill the resulting domain specific experts (agents) back into the base LLM, so that the whole system becomes recursively self-improving. We also hope to explore building up a library of reusable harnesses, and to apply our method to more challenging multimodal games, such as Craftax "
